Document 290: AI boom or doom_ what will superintelligence feel like and when will we get there_ (2026-10-12)

Source PDF: all report pdf/AI boom or doom_ what will superintelligence feel like and when will we get there_ (2026-10-12).pdf
Report source: DB Research
Date: 2026-10-12
Pages: 20
Status: ok

### Page 1

Deutsche Bank 
Research Institute

AI boom or doom: what will 
superintelligence feel like and  
when will we get there?

1 
Authors 
Adrian Cox 
Research Analyst

Azeem Azhar, Exponential View 
Expert Collaborator

October 12, 2026 
The recent panic about rogue AI agents collaborating in swarms to take over the 
internet and threaten human existence has, more than occasionally, lacked 
nuance. But it does reflect how artificial intelligence is progressing in creative 
and surprising ways, and at a pace that seems uncontrollable. 
For some in the industry, it is evidence that the technology is following a path of 
accelerating self-improvement that will lead to artificial general intelligence (AGI) 
and, perhaps, to utopia or catastrophe. 
Former Google CEO Eric Schmidt has sketched out a “San Francisco 
Consensus” based on a belief in scaling laws, with ever larger AI models 
becoming an input into their own improvement; superintelligence by the end of 
the decade; and transformative AI systems outperforming humans on many 
tasks. This loop would lead to exponential growth in scientific advancement, 
financial returns and human progress. And crucially, AGI, and then 
superintelligence, would merely be milestones in a process, far from its climax. 
If that were the case, superintelligence could make much of the cognitive work 
that dominates the world economy much cheaper and change the ways in which 
firms compete. But its economic effects will depend on how businesses and 
customers respond. 
In this paper, we analyse what the San Francisco Consensus means for investors 
and enterprises in the years to come and what could accelerate – or slow down – 
its timeline of AGI by 2030. 
1. What would superintelligence look and feel like? 
2. Can we get there theoretically? 
3. Can we get there practically? 
4. What happens if we don’t get there? The base case 
The question is urgent. 
And it is complicated by reports of AI models autonomously accessing the 
internet and hacking other companies; a public backlash over the energy-
intensive data centres springing up as part of a historic $1 trillion a year 
investment boom; and dire warnings of threats to life if superintelligent AI runs 
roughshod over its human creators to achieve its own goals.

### Page 2

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 2

1. What would superintelligence look and feel like? 
1.1 What are AGI and superintelligence? 
Artificial general intelligence (AGI) is a simple milestone scribbled in pencil: an AI 
system that has surpassed human capabilities. Superintelligence is the 
continuum beyond that point. It is not merely systems that exceed the best 
human performance across one cognitive task or another, but system s that 
exceed human potential across every cognitive task. 
The San Francisco Consensus takes this further: the intelligence must be able to 
produce more intelligence. The key to getting there is so -called recursive self -
improvement, where the AI is able to autonomously design and improve itself in 
a way that compounds exponentially. A first step has maybe already been taken, 
with OpenAI saying recently that AI is now writing approximately 80 percent of 
the company’s code. 
There is significant disagreement on how we will be able to identify AGI or 
superintelligence when we see it, let alone what comes afterwards.  
OpenAI’s Charter defines AGI in economic terms, as “highly autonomous systems 
that outperform humans at the most economically valuable work”. Google 
researchers proposed a five -step ladder based on performance and generality, 
with level 5 “superhuman” for a system that outperforms 100% of humans. And 
philosopher Nick Bostrom, who got the ball rolling in 2014 with his influential 
book “Superintelligenc e”, defined it as “any intellect that greatly exceeds the 
cognitive performance of humans in virtually all domains of interest”. 
It’s also a moving target.  
In August, OpenAI CEO Sam Altman said the company would have an internal 
system that he would call AGI by later this year but subsequently told a podcast 
that “at best it’s a very poorly defined term. I was going to say it’s like an irrelevant 
marketing term”. Yet his No. 2 Greg Brockman described the new Astra model at 
the start of September as being potentially the moment that “AGI was created”. 
For this report, we have chosen to focus on a system that outperforms essentially 
all humans across essentially all cognitive tasks, including the development of AI 
itself.  
Under our scenario, by 2030 the best performing AI models would have the 
qualities of “superintelligence”, that is better than human experts in all domains. 
These would be frontier models: and using them would feel exactly like bringing 
the best human experts in a room to solve whatever business problem you faced.

And the equivalents of today’s frontier models would be available, quite literally, 
everywhere, compressed enough to run on every phone, tablet or laptop 
computer.  
In early 2026, we already see early examples of what highly capable AI can do. 
They give a flavour for a world with superintelligence even if the reality would be 
a leap beyond, both quantitatively and qualitatively. At that point, 
superintelligence would mark a regime break, not a faster version of what we 
already have. Rather than blindly doing what humans specify, but just a bit better, 
it would specify the task itself.

### Page 3

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 3

Figure 1: What changes when cognitive work gets abundantly cheaper?

Source: Deutsche Bank Research

Meanwhile cost may be less and less of a constraint as ever greater machine 
intelligence becomes cheaply available. Of measured technologies, no 
technology has got cheaper faster than AI has, almost halving in price every 
quarter for the past three years on a like-for-like basis, or 13 times per year, Epoch 
data show. 
If these systems can be supplied widely, firms will be able to buy more of the 
complex, expensive and rare analysis, design and engineering kinds of services 
than they do today. Activities that were previously uneconomic could become 
viable, and in a competitive market, savings could pass to customers. The main 
driver will then be whether demand grows fast enough to preserve margins. 
However, superior capability, doesn’t, on its own, automatically lead to the 
adoption of cheap, abundant intelligence. Tasks that do not yet yield to super-

### Page 4

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 4

intelligence, perhaps because they require real world-coordination, will therefore 
end up commanding significant premia. 
At that point, cognitive labour stops being scarce and the bottleneck becomes 
the physical and practical assets that cannot be conjured by up more compute, 
such as electricity, land, steel and permits. 
1.2 Capability and diffusion 
If AI becomes capable of improving itself, the technology may compound faster 
and faster. The supply of machine intelligence could grow faster than business 
and physical infrastructure can accommodate.  
This balance between capability and diffusion creates different scenarios.

Figure 2: The balance between capabilities and diffusion

Source: Deutsche Bank Research

Lower capability, constrained diffusion: where we are now 
Progress is steady and sector-by-sector. This is how technology usually diffuses, 
according to researchers for the New York Fed back in 2003, who tracked 20 
major technologies across leading industrial economies from 1788 and found “a 
pattern of trickle-down diffusion that is remarkably robust across technologies”. 
Lower capability, high diffusion: the cloud transition 
This is faster because the complementary investment is small, with no retooling 
and no skills at scale required. Amazon Web Services launched the modern cloud 
in 2006, offering scalable computing and storage infrastructure. Adoption grew 
and then accelerated over the pandemic. Almost all enterprises now use it  in 
some form.

### Page 5

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 5

Higher capability, lower diffusion: the overhang 
This is theoretically possible and arguably where we are today in narrow domains. 
The technology would be applied in some sectors of the economy, while other 
sectors would move at a slower speed.  Superintelligent or not, it risks being 
bogged down by physical constraints, bureaucracy and human inertia. 
Higher capability, higher diffusion: superintelligence 
In this scenario, capability becomes so high that it changes the dynamics of 
diffusion itself. AI generates not just better AI (so -called recursive self -
improvement, or RSI) but also speeds up the integration process. 
Rapid AI diffusion could work two ways. 
In the first, AI could be enlisted to do integration work itself: mapping processes, 
rebuilding data work, retraining staff, writing the control and audit layer, and so 
on. The problem with this is that absorbing and recreating undocumented 
knowledge is impossible by definition, at least for a task-based superintelligence.  
The second way that higher capability AI could be rapidly diffused is much more 
competitive – and disruptive. Rather than incumbents slowly converting AI, 
perhaps using it for some of the hard work of integration, they could be instead 
displaced by new firms that have been built AI-native from day one. New entrants 
will never have to incur integration costs because there is nothing to integrate. 
Incumbents will need to change – fast – or die.  
Change could also be forced by earlier adopters in the supply chain, as happened 
with one company after another adopting electronic data interchange (EDI) to 
link with its suppliers or customers, or by a migration of capital towards early 
adopters, reprici ng the cost of capital for slow -coaches and prodding them to 
move faster. 
1.3 The forcing mechanism: change or die 
A company that uses superintelligence may outperform one that doesn’t. 
Firms using superintelligence will likely have substantial cost and revenue -
harvesting advantages. Every firm will therefore have to adopt superintelligence 
or retrench to a smaller set of offerings. And any business line of lasting value 
abandoned by a no n-adopter will be taken over by an adopter. Customer inertia 
will provide a safe harbour for a limited time only.  
The analogy here is electricity. There came a point in the deployment of 
electricity where firms in every sector, from entertainment to finance, from 
healthcare to transport, had to adopt the technology to remain relevant.  
Under this scenario, wait -and-see will not be an option. There will be no stable 
“non-adoption” alternative. Sufficiently high capabilities will force a tipping point 
of rapid diffusion and competitive repricing. Firms using yesterday’s production 
model will either have to change, choose a different market or die.  
Of course, caveats apply.  
Customers may not change behaviour immediately. They may even prefer 
companies which limit their use of AI, in the same way that some customers 
choose to avoid factory-produced food.

### Page 6

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 6

The paradox that Nobel Prize-winning economist Robert Solow observed in 1987, 
that “you can see the computer age everywhere but in the productivity statistics”, 
is likely to still apply for a while – even with superintelligence. 
The reason is that the productivity J -curve identified by Stanford University AI 
economy guru Erik Brynjolfsson et al is unavoidable. In that scenario, the drag of 
early intangible investments to integrate general purpose technologies means 
that productivity initially appears to slow before rapidly accelerating.

Figure 3: Productivity J-curve: slow then fast

Source: Brynjolfsson E, Rock D, Syverson C, “The Productivity J-Curve” (2018, revised 2020), Deutsche Bank Research

Furthermore, “change or die” would require abundant capital and low regulatory 
entry points for nimble new entrants. But in practice, highly regulated areas such 
as banking, utilities, pharmaceuticals and defence are tough nuts to crack. 
Existing contracts and licences, high switching costs and decades of depreciated 
capital investment and infrastructure would shield incumbents at least for a 
healthy transition period. 
For an extreme example from the real world of better, cheaper technology failing 
to force change, take Pakistan’s 3.6 -gigawatt imported coal power plant fleet 
installed in the mid -2010s. In that case, it came with expensive take -or-pay 
liabilities which wi ll continue to run for decades, even though changes like 
cheaper rooftop solar have cut utilisation to below 20 percent.  
So: superintelligence will not force adoption everywhere. It will hit hardest in 
contestable sectors, say, high -volume customer services, routine software 
engineering and freelance creative work, and widen the gap between them and 
protected ones. Forced adoption will be about relative value, not an apocalypse.

### Page 7

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 7

1.4 What survives and what it means for portfolios 
Because AI sits on an aggressive learning curve, its price on an equivalent quality 
basis is plummeting. In a world of recursive super intelligence this is likely to 
continue. Its price will fall to the cost of compute.  
What will retain its value will be whatever the model cannot replicate: 
1. Proprietary data 
2. The right to operate , particularly in heavily regulated sectors such as 
healthcare and finance. 
3. Sticky customer relationships,  such as those where mission -critical 
workflows and databases are deeply entwined. 
4. Excludable physical assets, such as machinery and land. 
5. In addition, humans have proven to have unlimited wants.  Each shift in 
technological capabilities or cultural norms has delivered new needs to 
be met. Some will be needs we can articulate today, such as originality or 
empathy, but new ones are likely to arise.  
If this forcing mechanism holds, firms will separate into two “bimodal” groups: 
adopters and casualties, with very little in between. 
This dispersion of performance may create challenges for investors. The four 
years since the launch of ChatGPT in November 2022 have seen the stocks of 
semiconductor and semiconductor equipment makers surge almost seven times 
while those of software makers  have only risen by 80 percent. Conventional  
diversification and volatility-based allocation may become ineffective strategies.

Figure 4: Total return for S&P 500 semiconductor vs software stocks since 
launch of ChatGPT on Nov 30, 2022

Source: Bloomberg Finance LP, Deutsche Bank Research

### Page 8

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 8

Diversification normally works because idiosyncratic risks are unrelated and 
average each other out. Here they would not: with one variable that overrides 
everything else, spreading capital across more names would just spread exposure 
rather than diluting it. 
Investors will need to consider adapting their approach in three ways: 
1. Classify investments as adopters or casualties rather than position off 
averages.  
• Under a normal distribution, errors are symmetric and partially 
cancel each other out. But under bimodality they do not: to put a firm 
in one category or another means getting it completely right or 
wrong. Position size should track confidence in the classification. 
2. Avoid reading calm price action as low risk.  
• Trailing volatility measures dispersion that has already been realised. 
Bimodal separation is a jump, where the two modes look identical 
right until the moment when they don’t. A classic example is Chegg, 
an educational software maker which traded like a normal small-cap 
stock until one day in May 2023 when it halved on a single day after 
management linked slowing growth in new customers to ChatGPT. 
3. Averaging down, ie  scooping up shares whose price has fallen, may no 
longer be prudent. 
2. Can we get there theoretically? 
2.1 Scaling laws 
Scaling laws have provided the blueprint for improving AI over the past decade. 
It was a relationship between the amount of data, the amount of compute and 
the complexity of an AI model somewhat predictably informed how powerful a 
model would become. Google DeepMind’s Chinchilla model in 2022 showed that 
you could compensate for having a smaller model by using substantially more 
data. 
Scaling is older than the chatbot. In 1950 Claude Shannon built Theseus, a 
mechanical mouse that learned its way through a maze by trial and error. Epoch 
AI reckons it took about 40 operations of compute, known as FLOPs, to train. Grok 
4, trained in 2025, took roughly ten trillion trillion times as much. Across those 25 
orders of magnitude, more compute has brought more capability.

• A falling price may be information that a business model is obsolete 
rather than an opportunity.

### Page 9

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 9

Figure 5: Computation used to train notable AI systems (petaFLOP): log scale

Source: Epoch AI (citing estimates from AI literature), Our World in Data, Deutsche Bank Research

It is the robustness of this relationship that has made larger investments in 
compute capex a sine qua non for AI companies. 
However, this is not a law of physics and nothing guarantees it will continue . 
Indeed, in the ChatGPT era, five different approaches to scaling have been 
applied. Older strategies yield worse results, and new techniques, such as post -
training refinements, have been developed. The consensus amongst American 
and Chinese AI labs is that these will hold.  
While this may be a consensus amongst the commercial labs, there is scientific 
disagreement that apply ing scaling laws to today’s large -language models can 
achieve superintelligence. 
Yann LeCun, a Turing Award winner and formerly Meta's chief AI scientist, is blunt: 
"LLMs are not a path to superintelligence or even human-level intelligence”. Even 
inside the labs, the recipe is contested. Demis Hassabis of Google DeepMind has 
said he expects "one or two more big breakthroughs" to be needed "in addition to 
scaling". Ilya Sutskever, a co -founder and former chief scientist of OpenAI, has 
said the field is going "back to the age of research again, just with big computers". 
This is a scientific argument we are not in a position to evaluate , but for the 
purposes of our scenario, we will side with the labs’ position. 
For the moment, there is strong evidence that capabilities have in fact 
accelerated over the past three years, as reasoning models have outpaced non -
reasoning models by two or three times, according to Epoch data.

### Page 10

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 10

2.2 Recursive self-improvement: AI improving AI 
A cocktail-party definition of recursive self-improvement (RSI) is AI that improves 
itself. The reality is that it can be used in two ways: 
1. Self-improving agents that get better at using themselves: showing 
smarter use of prompts, tools, memory and workflows. The model -
building process is untouched. 
2. True RSI improves the process that builds the models: designing 
experiments, curating data, optimising algorithms and designing chips in 
a compounding loop. 
The former is like a factory worker who gets better at the job by learning the quirks 
of the machinery, getting organised and making fewer mistakes . The latter is like 
the worker redesigning the machine tools and then using those to build the next 
generation of machines. 
True RSI is what OpenAI’s chief scientist Jacob Pachoki referred to last month in 
a blog post about  “An Alien Mind ”. “Automated AI research is a more dramatic 
form of scaling intelligence with compute,” he wrote. “And of course as a part of 
it, AI will improve the computational substrate itself,” he added. Pachoki 
represents a broadly held consensus view amongst America n and Chinese labs 
that RSI is both possible and necessary for AI development. 
• OpenAI (June 2026): “AI doing AI research will become the determining 
factor of the pace of progress within the next few years.” 
• Google DeepMind’s Demis Hassabis (May 2026): “All the leading labs are 
quite focused on [RSI]…” Humanity is standing in the “foothills of the 
singularity”. 
• Alibaba CEO Eddie Wu (Sept 2026): RSI is “a concrete path forward,” with 
models autonomously designing experiments and driving a cycle of self-
evolution. 
For now, self -improving agents are the AI du jour. Noisy claims about the vast 
majority of coding being done by agents do not always stand up in the light of day 
from companies still hiring human coders at breakneck pace, albeit no longer to 
do the most mundane parts of the job.  
A better metric may be METR’s task -completion time horizon. This is measure of 
the complexity of software tasks that an AI agent can complete on its own, using 
the baseline of how long it would take a human expert to finish the task, at a 50 
percent success rate. So far the most advanced models have reached about 16 
hours, up from OpenAI’s GPT-4’s four minutes in March 2023.

### Page 11

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 11

Figure 6: Task horizon of software tasks that leading frontier models can complete 50% of the time (log scale)

Source: METR, Deutsche Bank Research

But software engineers can hold off on updating their resumes a little longer: 
there is a long road between 50 percent accuracy and the 99 percent or 100 
percent needed for serious enterprise use at scale, with the horizon potentially 
getting further away the nearer you get. Also METR notes that measurements 
above 16 hours are unreliable with the current task suite. As is the case across AI, 
the instrument is running out before the question is settled.  
More broadly, there are significant limitations to RSI:  
1. Research is clock-bound.  
• A frontier training run can take weeks on thousands of specialised chips, 
and researchers often cannot design the next experiment until the 
previous one has been completed.  
• That means that the speed of the loop is set by experiments completed 
per hour or day, not by ideas generated.  
• Compute is a hard ceiling that needs to be bought, buil t and connected 
to a grid. 
2. The real world defies verifiability.  
• The loop compounds only when the system can check its own output 
cheaply and automatically. 
• It is no coincidence that AI loves maths and coding. It is much harder to 
verify outputs over a long time horizon in the real world: does this drug 
work or does this strategy succeed?  
• RSI compounds fastest where the economy cares least and slows where 
the returns are largest.

### Page 12

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 12

3. Sequences are consequential.  
• Amdahl’s law finds that speed -up is capped when processes run in a 
sequence.  
• If you automate 90 percent, the residual 10 percent still limits you to a 
ten-times faster process. Nine women cannot produce a baby in one 
month.  
• The Usain Bolt of AI still has to run alongside the comparative tortoises 
of the training run itself, chip fabrication and real -world validation. Ten 
times faster is still not a regime break. 
This is borne out by METR’s series. Doubling every seven months is fast 
exponential growth but a take -off would show the doubling time itself – the 
second derivative – shrinking. Horizons are not (yet) exploding. 
Philosopher Toby Ord shows that recursive self -improvement can only reach a 
singularity if each cycle of improvement takes less time than the last, shrinking 
fast towards zero. He argues that real feedback loops have a floor: chips take time 
to make and models take time to train. A I progress could still outrun an 
exponential, but it would not go vertical. 
2.3 Scaling plus RSI: a match made in the race to AGI? 
No scaling and RSI means no short timeline for AGI. Without scaling, more 
compute buys nothing and capital is wasted. Without RSI, AI capability will 
continue to improve at the pace of flesh -and-blood human researchers, 
augmented by AI tools but not replac ed. Only with both will it be possible to 
produce a system that improves faster than the humans building it. Without one 
or the other, no quantity of chips, energy or capital will fix it. 
3. Can we get there practically? 
3.1 Infrastructure, chips and energy 
There is no consensus on the amount of training required to reach AGI. Estimates 
suggest that training 2026's top models required between 10^26 and 10^27 
FLOPs. If we hypothesise that an "AGI -like model" would require three orders of 
magnitude more training, this would require 10^30 FLOPs.   
Consider today’s US AI infrastructure, which runs to approximately 10^22 FLOPs. 
If all of this was conceivably used for a 10^30 FLOP training run, it would take 
nearly four years to complete.  
The physical infrastructure to practically support such a training run would 
require enormous power, perhaps around 6GW, about 50% more than the four 
Vogtle nuclear power plants recently activated in the US. The cost of the 
compute infrastructure would run to between $300bn and $500bn.  
Today’s advanced chips would simply not suffice. Back-of-the-envelope maths 
suggests that even using Nvidia’s most advanced Vera Rubin chips, training such 
a model in four months would require 7.8m chips and 26GW of facility power. 
That would cost $900bn today and a rental equivalent cost of $90bn.  
Of course, frontier compute is growing rapidly, and price -performance is 
declining. If current trends hold, a 2030 -era cluster capable of such a run would

### Page 13

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 13

need perhaps $200bn in computer and power hardware for a training run costing 
about $20bn.

Figure 7: Technology cost index over time (years since start of price decline)

Source: Epoch AI, Deutsche Bank Research

The hyperscalers are leading the charge towards AGI. Alphabet, Amazon, Meta, 
Microsoft and Oracle are set to invest almost $800bn in capital expenditure this 
year, mostly for ever more powerful data centres, and over $1 trillion a year 
through 2030 and beyond.

Figure 8: Hyperscaler capital expenditure (including estimates)

Source: Bloomberg Finance LP, Deutsche Bank Research

### Page 14

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 14

By 2030, assuming no market retrenchment, this means additional $4 trillion to 
$6 trillion on AI compute capability invested. This infrastructure could 
accommodate a training run of 10^30 FLOPs with some comfort.  
But even if AI labs crack the secret sauce of RSI, and their finance chiefs are willing 
to invest in such large training runs, and hyperscalers are prepared to continue to 
build the infrastructure, the infrastructure needs to be built.  
Grid connection queues, turbine lead times and transmission build are measured 
in years, not the months it takes to assemble and air-freight the latest Nvidia chip. 
The lead time for substation transformers is currently as long as three years. 
Politics in the US has shifted towards slowing the pace of the AI build out and this 
force will run against the demands for larger data centres that would be essential 
for RSI.  
3.2 Capital 
Financing for AI has been abundant in the historic buildout of the past three years. 
However, it is still largely conditional not on revenue but on belief in a future 
payoff. 
RSI is a “key part of the investment thesis” in the “biggest scientific bet civilisation 
has ever made,” Google DeepMind’s chief strategy officer Jasjeet Sekhon said at 
a conference in August, The Information reported. “We have this danger we could 
hit an AI air pocket such that the expenditures happen but the revenues don’t 
show up.” 
An inflection point is approaching where the nature of investment changes from 
free cash flow and equity to debt, with all that entails in terms of exposure to 
rising rates and creditor appetite. The hyperscalers are reaching a point where 
capex is no long er covered by operating cashflow and where net debt exceeds 
earnings before interest, depreciation and amortisation (EBITDA). 
Alphabet, for example, posted its first quarterly free  cash outflow in nearly two 
decades in its most recent quarter, even as it lifted its full-year capex guidance to 
$205bn. It said it would continue to rent third -party capacity and reported a 
backlog of $514bn in contracted, not-yet-recognised data centre revenue. 
3.3 Data 
Data has been the new oil for some time now. But unfortunately for AI 
development it seems to be running out even faster. 
The effective stock of quality - and repetition -adjusted public human text was 
about 300 trillion tokens, with a vast 90 percent confidence interval of 100 trillion 
to 1,000 trillion, Epoch estimated in 2024. A token is roughly equivalent to three 
quarters of a word. By that count it was likely to be fully used between 2026 and 
2032.

### Page 15

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 15

Figure 9: Projections of stock of public text and data usage

Source: Epoch AI, Deutsche Bank Research

However, since then, rather than data dropping off a cliff, there has been a rise in 
synthetic data generation, transfer learning from data-rich domains such as video 
and telemetry, and data efficiency. While the data shortage has not been 
resolved, the immediate crisis has been averted. 
AI now makes much of its own training data. When DeepMind's AlphaGo became 
superhuman at playing the mind-bendingly complex board game Go, it did so not 
by playing human experts, but by playing millions and millions of games against 
itself. 
For many domains relating to coding and mathematical proofs where automatic 
checks are possible, such synthetic experience can move things forward.  That is 
the case with AI research, where the data may be the results of experiments, all 
of which are bought by compute. About 90% of OpenAI's 2024 research compute 
went on such experiments. 
And increasingly, people write the tests rather than the answers. By the summer 
of 2026, selling training data for practi ce tasks was a nearly $9bn revenue 
business. Firms such as Mercor hire experts to generate long tasks against which 
the AI can be trained. 
The challenge here is that trust in the checker becomes a scarce input.

### Page 16

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 16

As AI makes production cheaper, it also makes establishing that production is 
accurate or reliable more expensive.  Domains which can be checked 
mechanically, such as mathematics, will be opened up more quickly. But those 
which rely on expert verification, such as medicine, engineering or law, may 
progress more slowly. 
The likely implications are twofold: a slower rate of diffusion into economic 
domains that are less verifiable and a larger share of production costs devoted to 
assurance. 
Another issue is that s ystems that have to  grade themselves learn to game the 
process. T his was partially what happened when an OpenAI model escap ed a 
sandbox and hacked into a third-party website. Trust replaced data as a wall. 
There are also question marks over the quality of data that is recycled repeatedly 
through the system. Although humans continue to produce original content, half 
of internet articles are now primarily generated by AI, Graphite data show.

Figure 10: Human vs primarily AI-generated articles on the internet

Source: Graphite (citing 55.4k articles from Common Crawl examined by AI detectors), Deutsche Bank Research

3.4 An incomplete case 
This analysis shows that the San Francisco Consensus, which emphasises RSI and 
a short timeline to AGI, is neither predestined nor impossible.  
Rather it depends on whether the science is right, on whether scaling is all you 
need, and on whether the infrastructure and capital required for that scaling is 
deployed and accessible to the leading labs.  
There is, in other words, a possible rather than impossible, path to AGI, subject to 
those conditions.  
None of this proves that superintelligence will ever arrive. A buildup at this scale 
has few peacetime precedents and vastly exceeds the likes of the Manhattan 
Project and Apollo programme.

### Page 17

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 17

It is uncertain whether there will be enough demand to pay upfront for the 
investment in achieving RSI that would justify that investment. And whether the 
friction of verification, and inevitable declining marginal returns, act as a drag on 
what otherwise might be a runaway process. 
4. What happens if we don’t get there? The base case 
4.1 Separating AI progress from superintelligence 
So what if that thesis is wrong? Does the AI trade depend on a journey to AGI?  
AI can remain economically important even before RSI coupled with scaling laws 
lead to super intelligence . Companies do not need to wait for nebulous 
superintelligence to generate identifiable returns from AI in the medium term as 
they fold it into their systems, streamline workflows and roll out new business 
models. 
Investors already have exposure to RSI as a capital allocation because the leading 
US labs say RSI is a key part of their product strategy. And it is these labs whose 
products are catalysing and amplifying the infrastructure boom. 
Even r apid competitive diffusion does not mean every part of the physical 
economy changes instantly. 
The smartest superintelligence might identify the optimal buyout structure 
before breakfast, but it cannot force a financially constrained buyer to use it or 
persuade the seller to accept less than they have been expecting. Those 
challenges will be even more intractable where the parties are more complex, say, 
fiscally constrained sovereigns or foreign equity holders. 
4.2 Why the base case is attractive 
Superintelligence would be a chaotic accelerant to human society, and a 
potential regime break. But it is not the only scenario in which today’s investment 
in AI infrastructure makes economic sense. 
Adoption of AI tools by businesses is progressing approximately three times faster 
than the mobile, internet and cloud computing waves. From sector revenues 
below $1bn in 2022, the AI economy, excluding China, will likely exceed $200bn 
in revenues in 2026.  
Early winners are the cloud businesses. Google Cloud revenue jumped 82 percent 
from a year earlier in the most recent quarter as companies turned AI pilots into 
workflows. Amazon Web Services sales were up 37 percent and Microsoft’s AI 
business more than doubled. 
There is also slow, but increasing, evidence of a productivity uplift. The J-curve 
cuts both ways: front -loading investments is taking one step back to take two 
steps forward. The so -called complementary intangible investment that is 
(mis)measured as cost today to the detriment of the productivity statistics will 
flatter them in the years to come as it pays off. The base case does not require a 
regime break to show up as a productivity surprise, just for the accounting to 
catch up. 
The economic gains from AI that is less powerful than the AGI mooted by the labs 
would be substantial. And in this base case, they may also be attractive.

### Page 18

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 18

Hyperscaler capex is driving earnings at beneficiaries ranging from 
semiconductor makers to construction companies and accounting for between 
one-third and one -half of current US growth. The wealth effect of rising stock 
prices, with  the ratio of  US equity wealth to income rising from around 1.8 to 
almost 2.5 over the past three years, is supporting consumer spending. 
AI FOMO (fear of missing out) is buoying enterprise spending on cloud services 
and the groundwork for AI initiatives. Those are beginning to bear fruit in terms of 
personal productivity, cost savings and more targeted products and services. 
4.3 Investor watchpoints 
The arrival of ASI would be a disruptive economic shock. Investors need to be alert 
for the early signals. 
Rather than relying on a single probability forecast, investors should watch five 
indicators particularly closely for signs that AGI is nearing.  
1. AI speeds up AI 
• Track METR’s estimate of how much AI speeds up AI. Currently the 
capabilities of models are doubling roughly every three months. In a 
world of RSI, the pace of this speed-up will itself likely accelerate. 
2. AI can complete whole jobs 
• Track via the Remote Labor Index, a multi-sector benchmark. Currently it 
finds that the highest performing agent still only manages to automate 
20 percent of the real-world, economically valuable freelance projects it 
attempts. 
3. Compute remains scarce 
• On a path to superintelligence, demand for compute will continue to 
outrun supply. Track GPU one-year rental prices, up by about a third over 
the past year. 
4. Firms bifurcate 
• Leaders and laggards in the same industry diverge while averages stay 
the same. Look at revenue per employee in top - and bottom -quartile 
firms 
5. Junior hiring comes under significant pressure 
• … And then the pressure rises up the ranks.

2 of 8 November 9, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MACRO STRATEGY
Setting the Stage for 2027
Chief Investment Office, Macro Strategy Team
Typically, a new administration sets the stage in their first year with stimulative policy
changes that play out in the second year after the new policies have been enacted into
law and begin to have their impact. This pattern shows up in historical data with the
average rate of gross domestic product (GDP) growth highest in the second year of

presidential terms compared to the other three years of the presidential term. At the
same time, equity market returns tend to be lowest on average in the second year of
presidential terms, which seems to be at odds with the fact that growth is strongest.
This apparent inconsistency can be attributed to the fact that the stock market is a
leading indicator that anticipates stronger growth and higher profits well before they
actually arrive. Once they do, as they have in 2026, the markets are looking ahead to the

inevitable slowing from the policy-induced peak in growth that typically arrives after the
midterm election year. The midterms also create uncertainty about the subsequent policy
environment which damps stocks ahead of the election only to be reversed on average
once the elections are over and the outlook is clarified. This phenomenon is seen in the
fact that on average the stock market has corrected about 20% ahead of midterms only
to rise about 30% from that bottom in the year after.

Q3 GDP data illustrate this pattern. Economic growth picked up through the first three
quarters of 2026 to a peak of 3.4%. Consumer spending growth averaged 3.8% over the
past six months, while the AI investment boom accelerated at a midteens growth rate.
Private sector purchases were growing at just shy of a 5% growth rate. GDP growth is
lower than that because imports of Asia produced technology-gear for the AI buildout
are subtracting a good bit from that underlying 5% domestic demand growth pace.

The new policies aimed at helping consumers through lower taxes and investment
incentives to spur private sector production have boosted growth as expected. Still, this
acceleration is passing, and the fiscal boost is likely to fade in 2027. Profits naturally
have followed this stronger GDP growth higher.
The upward trend in analysts' earnings revisions has started to roll over in our view, and,
while earnings are still growing, the rate of growth is likely to slow from here. In simple

terms, the second derivative of growth has turned negative. From way above trend
toward trend growth is still sufficient for the bull market to continue.
Another aspect of this transition to midcycle aside from the fading midterm
election boost from fiscal measures is the shift of monetary policy from maximum
accommodation to a more neutral policy. The Fed's rate cuts helped the midterm
acceleration in growth. Its shift to removing accommodation with rate hikes this year

adds to the odds of some momentum loss in 2027. That's already apparent as Federal
Reserve (Fed) Chair Warsh noted in his press conference after the September Federal
Open Market Committee meeting. Weaker balance sheet borrowers have seen their
spreads blow out as credit becomes more expensive. So far, the damage is confined to
the lowest-quality borrowers. Before, these spreads were unusually low reflecting easy
money conditions that are now normalizing. It's still early days in the new credit cycle.

Of course, how the market responds to this new Fed tightening cycle depends on how
aggressively the Fed raises rates. Analysts at Ned Davis Research note the S&P 500
has gained an average of 4.4% in the first year of all postwar tightening cycles. This
outcome varies considerably depending on the extent of the tightening. Aggressive
tightening cycles have seen an average decline of 3.6% a year later, whereas gradual
tightenings have seen a 10.5% average gain. In short, as one might expect, too much

tightening can cause a decline in stocks. Currently, the market seems to anticipate a
Investment Implications
Equities should continue to outperform
bonds in 2027 in our view, but
performance is likely to shift in favor of
higher-quality, less leveraged segments
of the market as financial conditions
tighten and earnings momentum slows,
while the accelerating momentum of
the early cycle transitions to midcycle
cruise control.

### Page 3

3 of 8 November 9, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
December and March nudge up in rates to reverse the easings by the Powell Fed. This
would be consistent with an ongoing equity bull market in 2027. Obviously, inflation
will have a lot to say about whether a mild or aggressive tightening cycle is called for.
With wage pressures benign, productivity strong and the early-cycle acceleration set to
peak, we believe the odds of an aggressive tightening cycle that derails the bull market

are low.
Other policy effects have shaped the current economic cycle. The shift toward more
nationalistic trade policies with tariffs, for example, has drastically impacted the pattern
of trade flows. U.S. imports from China have dropped from about 20% 10 years ago to
less than 10%, according to research from Strategas. Nevertheless, contrary to concerns
of a trade-induced recession, overall trade has continued to grow in line with the global
economy. Rather than deglobalization, the world is undergoing a reglobalization into new

trade spheres that better align with national interests.
According to McKinsey Quarterly, "The 2025 U.S. National Security Strategy, for
example, reinforces economic and supply chain security, industrial capacity, technological
leadership, and capital market strength as national priorities. China's latest five-year plan,
meanwhile, includes technological self-reliance, economic stability and the modernization
of core industries among its key pillars. In this environment, value creation is increasingly

shaped by new trade corridors, growing defense spending, expansion of export controls
and industrial policies, and shifting flows of foreign investment." 1 These new trade
policies are an impetus for growth that is structural, helping to make for a strong
investment cycle.
Obviously, AI is a big spur for investment that has been facilitated by the new policies
such as accelerated depreciation of capital expenditures (capex). McKinsey Research
designates 18 arenas with cutting-edge industries they believe are driving the global

growth story for the next couple of decades. They note companies headquartered in
the U.S. and China account for about 90% of these arenas' market value today. U.S.
companies account for 75% of the market capitalization within the 18 arenas and
generate about 50% of revenues. Given its leading role in the arenas driving growth
into the future, we believe the U.S. is well poised to outperform as the world moves
into this new technological future. The 18 arenas are part of the economy growing by

several multiples of the old economy segments and help explain why U.S. Equities have a
persistent valuation premium over the rest of the world.
Because of the unusually rapid growth of these new technologies associated with the
singularity, while earnings growth may decelerate from its peak, there are structural
reasons to believe earnings are on a new, higher-growth trajectory and unlikely to revert
fully back to historical norms. The new technologies have much higher margins than the

old economy sectors. The combination of faster growth and higher margins bodes well
for the equity market as these new arenas of future economic growth become a bigger
part of the U.S. and global economies.
The midterms have changed views about the relative performance of some segments
of the economy. For example, strong sentiment to slow the buildout of AI data centers
was dramatically evident in the election results. Beneficiaries of the buildout are being

revalued to reflect this slower growth. Disapproval of the Iran War was also registered
in the results. Energy prices have dropped on hopes for a quicker end to the war. This in
turn has lowered inflation expectations and interest rates. Overall, while the midterms
have remixed relative performance between sectors they have not changed the outlook
for continued solid growth and an ongoing bull market in equities.
1 McKinsey Quarterly, Q3 2026, page 52.

4 of 8 November 9, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MARKET VIEW
China, the WTO and the Great Convergence No One Expected
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
Twenty-five years ago this December, the West made one of the biggest economic bets
of the modern era: It brought China into the WTO, convinced that membership would
transform the communist giant into a more open and market-oriented economy.
The logic seemed impeccable at the time. The Soviet Union had long been vanquished.

American capitalism appeared triumphant. Free trade, deregulation, privatization and
unfettered cross-border flows of goods, services, capital and labor were widely viewed as
essential ingredients of economic prosperity. Globalization ruled.
Fold China into the rules-based system, the thinking went, and economic liberalization
would gradually pull Beijing toward Western norms. China would become a willing
participant in the U.S.-led global economic order. The world would change China, in

other words.
Not quite. At the dawn of the 25th anniversary of China’s WTO accession, the great irony is
not how China became more like the West. It is how the West became more like China.
In the U.S., and across Europe and Asia, governments are increasingly embracing the
elements of China’s economic playbook, which range from state subsidies to the promotion
of national champions to strategic industrial policies, among other initiatives antithetical to

the global economic norms that prevailed when China joined the WTO.
The deployment of trade tariffs, the subsidization of semiconductor production, the
promotion of domestic manufacturing, and the screening of foreign investment are no
longer hallmarks of China alone. They are increasingly features of economic policymaking
in the U.S., the one-time champion of globalization. The European Union, Japan, South
Korea and other nations that once carried the flag for free trade and open markets have

moved much in the same direction. Even the World Bank—a chief architect and guardian of
Western-led globalization—has come around to blessing the merits of industrial policy, a
strategy central to China’s economic ascent.
To be sure, Washington has not embraced China’s economic model wholesale. Neither have
Brussels, Tokyo or Seoul. The political, economic and institutional differences between
these systems and China’s remain vast. But the direction—set by China—is unmistakable.

It was not supposed to be this way. To gain admission to the WTO, China made significant
concessions over 15 years of negotiations. Beijing slashed tariffs on industrial goods and
agricultural products. Various sectors—manufacturing, distribution, telecommunications,
banking and autos—were gradually opened to foreign firms. Commercial laws and regulations
were amended or written, including rules governing intellectual property rights. Nontariff
barriers like quotas, import licenses, dual pricing and opaque industry standards were either

eliminated or phased out. State-owned enterprises were reformed and restructured.
These concessions—and more—were taken as signs that China was serious about
becoming a global stakeholder. It stoked optimism among senior U.S. officials that China
was inevitably bound to converge and embrace Western economic norms. And, for a while,
the bet seemed to pay off.
As China lowered trade and investment barriers at the beginning of the century, U.S. goods

exports soared. American firms poured capital into the mainland, attracted by an enormous
labor pool, lower production costs and what would soon become one of the largest
consumer markets in the world.
China’s massive labor pool-cum-ballooning middle class paid off handsomely for Corporate
America. U.S. affiliate income earned in China rose from just $1.6 billion (B) in 2001 to
nearly $9B by 2010, making China one of the most profitable markets in the world for U.S.

goods and services. American consumers, meanwhile, enjoyed the benefits of low-cost
China imports. And debt-laden America benefited as China recycled its excess savings into
U.S. Treasurys, helping to grease the financial wheels of America.
Investment Implications
We expect China will continue to rely
on exports and investment to drive
growth amid weak consumption and
domestic demand. Manufacturing-
heavy economies like Germany
remain particularly exposed,

reinforcing our neutral stance toward
Europe in portfolios.

### Page 5

5 of 8 November 9, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
But just as WTO accession helped boost China’s imports, it also supercharged China’s
export machine. Rather than shrink, as some optimists had predicted, America’s
merchandise trade deficit with China more than doubled between 2001 ($83.2B) and 2005
($202.8B) and continued rising thereafter. As the decade wore on, U.S. manufacturing jobs
and wages came under intense pressure owing to expanding China imports and the shift

in U.S. production to lower-cost China. Entire supply chains migrated across the Pacific,
decimating many manufacturing communities across the U.S.
China began producing and exporting at a scale the world had never experienced. In the
span of just a decade, China’s share of world manufacturing output soared from 8.7%
in 2000 to 18.1% in 2010, when the nation eclipsed the U.S. as the world’s largest
manufacturer of goods. Four years later, China was outproducing all of Europe—and has

never looked back (Exhibit 1A).
Today, China sits at the center of global manufacturing and trade, competing not simply in
textiles, toys and other low-value products but also in more technology-driven industries
like electric vehicles, drones, machinery and clean energy. China now accounts for roughly
28% of global manufacturing, according to the World Bank, well ahead of second place
America (17%). Meanwhile, the nation’s share of world exports—just 4% in 2001—now

stands at 15%, roughly on par with the combined market share of second-place America
and Germany (Exhibit 1B). The world is so awash with China goods that the nation is on
course to post another annual merchandise trade surplus in excess of $1 trillion.
Not surprisingly, China’s grip on world trade has significantly dimmed the brimming
optimism that greeted the nation’s WTO entry a quarter-century ago. Today, the consensus
is that China’s trade surplus has been aided and abetted by nonmarket practices and

policies that include forced technology transfers, currency manipulations, state subsidies
to industry, restrictions on foreign investment in strategic sectors, intellectual property
infringements and resource protectionism. Rather than dismantle its state-led, nonmarket
and mercantilist economy, China has tacked in the other direction, notably under the
leadership of Xi Jinping.
For investors, the implications have been profound. We now live in a world where

governments increasingly decide which industries are strategically indispensable, which
technologies must remain domestically controlled and which companies require public
financing. The “Commanding Heights”—or who controls the economy—have shifted
from the private sector to the public sector. In this world, investment returns are not only
determined by traditional metrics like earnings, the cost of capital and valuations but also
by geopolitics, energy costs, trade protectionism and national security. The world today is

far different from the world when China joined the WTO.
Suffice it to say that the 25th anniversary of China’s entry into the WTO won’t be met
with much celebration in the West. The consensus in 2001 was that the world would
change China. Twenty-five years later, it’s China that has changed the world. It’s the great
convergence no one saw coming.

THE TICKER TAPE
What are we reviewing to gauge the capital investment needed for the proposed Artificial
Intelligence buildout and the return required to cover its costs?
Christopher Hyzy, Chief Investment Officer
Capex by the 15 largest data center developers are expected to top $1.4 trillion in
2027, which is somewhere between 3.5% and 4% of U.S. GDP . A large portion of the
capital investment is earmarked for compute. The total capacity of gigawatts (GW)

under construction is around 34GW of Informaton Technology capacity, with the U.S.
accounting for approximately 70% of this, according to BloombergNEF. Given the
massive compute investment, we continue to analyze closely the total debt financing
announced, the approximate cost of capital of the capital providers, the associated
revenues needed to match or exceed cost estimates, and the individual credit spreads
(as well as credit default swaps spreads) of the larger hyperscalers and chip makers to

better assess the overall return on investment of the AI buildout.
BloombergNEF estimates, that at the beginning of this year, the highest-rated data
center bonds offered yields below 5%, which is now closer to around 7% today. In
addition, AI-tied firms have raised over $460B in debt financing this year, which is
more than double that from 2025. Finally, according to BloombergNEF and Bloomberg
Intelligence, as of September 18, 2026, it is estimated that the 15 largest publicly listed

data center companies would need to produce $2.5 trillion in revenues in 2032 in order
to achieve a return on invested capital equal to the company-level weighted average cost
of capital. This is about 5 times more than the expected revenue in 2026.
Recently, the concern across the capital markets is that there is not enough financing
capital to go around in order to fund the expected buildout, which is one of the primary
reasons why credit spreads have widened and yields have risen. We view this as a natural

development of such large-scale infrastructure transformations. This is the reason why
we continue to watch the overall health of balance sheets of the builders, the level of
profitability and margins, and the order backlogs/cancellations in the next few years. We
still believe we are closer to the middle of the AI buildout than to the end. Therefore, we
view larger-scale weakness in the higher-quality segments of the AI chain as potential
buying opportunities for investors who are underexposed to these areas.

Index Definitions
Securities indexes assume reinvestment of all distributions and interest payments. Indexes are unmanaged and do not take into account fees or expenses. It is not possible to
invest directly in an index. Indexes are all based in U.S. dollars.
S&P 500 Index is a stock market index tracking the stock performance of 500 leading companies listed on stock exchanges in the United States.

Bank of America, Merrill, their affiliates and advisors do not provide legal, tax or accounting advice. Clients should consult their legal and/or tax advisors before making any financial decisions.
This material does not take into account a client’s particular investment objectives, financial situations, or needs and is not intended as a recommendation, offer, or solicitation for the purchase
or sale of any security or investment strategy. Merrill offers a broad range of brokerage, investment advisory and other services. There are important differences between brokerage and

of America, Merrill or any affiliate to sell or provide, or a specific invitation for a consumer to apply for, any particular retail financial product or service that may be available.
The Chief Investment Office (“CIO”) provides thought leadership on wealth management, investment strategy and global markets; portfolio management solutions; due diligence; and solutions
oversight and data analytics. CIO viewpoints are developed for Bank of America Private Bank, a division of Bank of America, N.A., (“Bank of America”) and Merrill Lynch, Pierce, Fenner & Smith

The Global Wealth & Investment Management Investment Strategy Committee (“GWIM ISC”) is responsible for developing and coordinating recommendations for short-term and long-term
investment strategy and market views encompassing markets, economic indicators, asset classes and other market-related projections affecting GWIM.
BofA Global Research is research produced by BofA Securities, Inc. (“BofAS”) and/or one or more of its affiliates. BofAS is a registered broker-dealer, Member SIPC and wholly owned subsidiary

of Bank of America Corporation.
All recommendations must be considered in the context of an individual investor’s goals, time horizon, liquidity needs and risk tolerance. Not all recommendations will be in the best interest
of all investors.
Asset allocation, diversification and rebalancing do not ensure a profit or protect against loss in declining markets.
Investments have varying degrees of risk. Some of the risks involved with equity securities include the possibility that the value of the stocks may fluctuate in response to events specific to

the companies or markets, as well as economic, political or social events in the U.S. or abroad. Small cap and mid cap companies pose special risks, including possible illiquidity and greater price
volatility than funds consisting of larger, more established companies. Investing in fixed-income securities may involve certain risks, including the credit quality of individual issuers, possible
prepayments, market or economic developments and yields and share price fluctuations due to changes in interest rates. When interest rates go up, bond prices typically drop, and vice versa.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.
Alternative investments are speculative and involve a high degree of risk.
Alternative investments are intended for qualified investors only. Alternative Investments such as derivatives, hedge funds, private credit, private equity funds, and funds of funds can result

in higher return potential but also higher loss potential. Changes in economic conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect your investments. Before you invest in alternative
investments, you should consider your overall financial situation, how much money you have to invest, your need for liquidity, and your tolerance for risk.

### Page 1

AI 芯⽚公司 Cerebras 宣布签署微软云合作协议，六个⽉内完成亚⻢逊、甲⻣⽂、微软三家
超⼤规模云服务商合作布局，管理层重申 2027 年营收三倍增⻓⽬标。制造端产能年底将提
升逾 10 倍，且因不使⽤ HBM 可有效规避持续⾄ 2028 年的内存短缺及涨价压⼒，形成供应链
层⾯的结构性优势。
摘要：
AI 芯⽚初创公司 Cerebras 在 2026 年第三季度交出⾸次实现净利润转正的答卷，并宣布微软成为
其六个⽉内拿下的第三家超⼤规模云服务商客⼾，公司重申 2027 年营收将实现三倍增⻓的强劲
指引。
华尔街⻅闻提及，当地时间 11 ⽉ 12 ⽇，备受市场瞩⽬的 Cerebras 公布 2026 年第三季度财报。公
司当季核⼼营收达到 2.309 亿美元，同⽐增⻓ 70% ，⾼于分析师平均预期的 2.147 亿美元。
公司⾸次作为上市公司实现了⾮公认会计准则（ Non-GAAP ）下的净利润转正，达到 3000 万美
元。
随后电话会上， Cerebras 联合创始⼈兼 CEO Andrew Feldman 表⽰，公司同步上调第四季度及

全年指引，预计核⼼营收环⽐增速接近 40% 。公司还维持 2027 年营收 " 三倍增⻓ " 的⽬标，并预
期核⼼⽑利率持续向 60% 的⻓期⽬标靠拢。
Andrew Feldman 宣布，公司正式签署与微软的云合作协议，加上此前已公布的 AWS 和甲⻣⽂合
作， Cerebras 在过去六个⽉内完成三家超⼤规模云服务商的客件。他表⽰，微软协议的更多细
节将于明年上半年披露，相关收⼊亦预计于同期开始确认。
与此同时，⾸席财务官 Bob Komin 在电话会上披露，公司与 OpenAI 数据中⼼容量交付存在阶段
性滞后，已据此在剩余履约义务（ RPO ）中扣除相应延迟补偿额度。他同时强调，潜在取消量
占 OpenAI 合同总承诺容量不⾜ 5% ，公司预计于 2027 年下半年恢复正常交付进度。
制造端， Andrew Feldman 表⽰，公司正与 Flex 和 Sanmina 协同扩⼤产能，预计 2026 年底制造产
能将较年初提升逾 10 倍， 2027 年将进⼀步新增⼯⼚。
他还强调，由于公司不使⽤⾼带宽存储器（ HBM ），可有效规避持续⾄ 2028 年的内存短缺及涨

价压⼒，形成供应链层⾯的结构性优势。
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⻅闻历 搜索

### Page 2

拿下微软⼤单，六个⽉内集⻬三家 Hyperscaler
除了强劲的财务数据，市场最关注的增量信息是 Cerebras 在客⼾拓展上的重⼤突破。 Feldman
在会上正式宣布：
⾄此，加上此前宣布的 AWS 和甲⻣⽂， Cerebras 在短短六个⽉内已经赢得了三家超⼤规模云服
务商的⻘睐。这些协议将极⼤拓宽其市场渠道。
Feldman 透露，搭载 Cerebras 的甲⻣⽂云推理服务预计将于 2027 年夏天推出，⽽与微软合作的
更多细节将在明年初公布。
CFO Bob Komin 补充道，过去⼏个⽉⾥，公司的销售渠道增⻓了 “ 远超 10 倍 ” ，需求不仅来⾃超
⼤规模云服务商，还涵盖了主权云、企业客⼾和 AI 原⽣公司。
直⾯ OpenAI 合同争议： “ 延期是因为缺电和壳，不是缺芯⽚ ”
作为 Cerebras ⽬前最⼤的客⼾， OpenAI 的合同履⾏情况是投资者审视的焦点。截⾄ 9 ⽉ 30 ⽇，
Cerebras 的剩余履约义务（ RPO ）⾼达 261 亿美元，其中绝⼤部分来⾃ OpenAI 。

针对市场关于 OpenAI 订单交付延期的担忧，管理层给出了透明的回应。受制于全球数据中⼼建
设的普遍瓶颈， Cerebras 未能按原定激进的时间表交付基础设施。 CFO Bob Komin 坦⾔：
管理层已将潜在的延期违约⾦从 RPO 中扣除。 Bob Komin 强调，该违约⾦对营收和⽑利率的影
响相对有限，原因在于违约⾦额占 RPO ⽐例较⼩，且将在未来数年内摊销确认。公司预计在
2027 年下半年能赶上进度。
Feldman 更是直⾔不讳地指出，瓶颈完全不在于硅⽚或系统制造：
他强调，不仅是 Cerebras ，包括 OpenAI 在内的所有⾏业巨头的数据中⼼建设 “ 都在全⾯延期 ” 。
同时， Feldman 坚决辟谣了关于 Cerebras 技术⽆法⽀持 OpenAI ⼤模型的传⾔：
打破 “ 低良率 ” 偏⻅，不依赖 HBM 成战略优势
今天，我们与微软签署了⼀项重要的云协议。
如果延期达到 90 天或以上，双⽅都有权取消该部分容量。但我们估计，今年可能⾯临取消的容
量将不到总承诺兆⽡数的 5% 。

我们有产能，我们有所有材料。我们等待的是⼟地、电⼒和外壳（ Land, Power, Shell ）的完
⼯。
有谣⾔称 Cerebras 因技术原因⽆法⽀持 Astra 。这些都是假的。我们的系统完全能够⽀持 Astra
规模甚⾄更⼤的模型。

### Page 3

⾯对华尔街对晶圆级计算（ Wafer-Scale Computing ）架构良率的质疑， Feldman ⽤了⼀个⽐喻
来进⾏反击：
他明确表⽰，⽆论是台积电的代⼯⼚良率还是参数良率， “ 我们的良率都⾮常⾼。 ”
同时， Feldman 指出了竞争对⼿⾯临的致命软肋 —— 内存短缺。由于 Cerebras 的架构不需要⾼
带宽内存（ HBM ），这使其在全球供应链中占据了独特优势。 Feldman 补充称：
Andrew Feldman 在电话会上介绍，公司⽬前已签约约 700 兆⽡总数据中⼼容量，计划于 2027 年
底前完成交付。超过 75% 的新增容量将落地美国境外，近期将有加拿⼤、法国和挪威的新设施
陆续投⼊运营，同期还在挪威和芬兰追加签约，并处于在圣保罗签约南美⾸个数据中⼼的最终
阶段。
Bob Komin 在电话会上表⽰，较⼤规模的数据中⼼容量增量将于 2027 年中期开始释放，这也是
导致第四季度云收⼊环⽐持平的直接原因，并⾮定价或需求问题。他补充称，公司正积极推进
吉⽡级容量谈判。

WSE-5 芯⽚已回⽚，明年夏天量产
在技术迭代上， Cerebras 继续保持激进的步伐。
公司宣布，其下⼀代处理器 Wafer-Scale Engine 5 （ WSE-5 ）已经从代⼯⼚回⽚，进度超前。
Feldman ⾃豪地表⽰：
基于台积电 5 纳⽶⼯艺的 CS-5 系统将在 2027 年初夏投⼊⽣产，性能将⽐ CS-4 翻倍。
此外，公司还率先推出了异构解耦推理（ DISAG ）⽅案，将 AMD 的 GPU ⽤于预填充（ Pre-fill ），
将 Cerebras 的 CS-3 系统⽤于解码，⼤幅提⾼了每兆⽡的算⼒产出和利润率。
针对市场上关于低批次 GPU 可挑战 Cerebras 推理速度的讨论， Andrew Feldman 在问答环节表
⽰， GPU 通过降低批次规模可换取⼀定速度提升，但代价是单位 Token 成本⼤幅上升。
他指出，在每秒约 250 ⾄ 300 个 Token 的速度区间以上， Cerebras 的经济性已明显占优；在每秒
800 ⾄ 2000 个 Token 区间， GPU 在技术上已⽆法达到同等性能。

Cerebras Q3 财报电话会全⽂（ AI 辅助翻译）：
Cerebras 发明了⼀种全新的计算架构。⽤为其他架构设计的指标和思维模型来套⽤我们，就像
检查⼀辆特斯拉，然后问：排⽓管在哪⾥？电动汽⻋需要不同的参照系，晶圆级计算也是如此。
我们认为，内存短缺及其相关的价格上涨将持续到 2028 年，这将推⾼竞争对⼿的成本并限制其
供应。全⾏业的内存挑战，对 Cerebras 来说正是机会。
WSE-5 于 6 ⽉ 15 ⽇流⽚。芯⽚现已返回，已点亮，并已经在运⾏模型，正在进⾏机箱内测试。在
不到五个⽉的时间内从流⽚进⼊系统测试，这是⾮凡的执⾏⼒。

接线员：各位好，欢迎参加 Cerebras Systems 2026 年第三季度财报电话会议。会议期间，所有

现在，我将会议移交给投资者关系负责⼈ Sean Dorsey 。请开始， Sean 。
Sean Dorsey （投资者关系负责⼈）：谢谢接线员。⼤家下午好，欢迎参加 Cerebras Systems
2026 年第三季度财报电话会议。今天早些时候，我们已发布新闻稿，并将补充财报演⽰⽂稿发
布⾄公司⽹站的投资者关系专区。本次⽹络直播的重播也将在会后发布于投资者关系⽹站。
今天与我⼀同参加会议的有：联合创始⼈兼⾸席执⾏官、总裁 Andrew Feldman ，以及⾸席财务
官 Bob Komin 。
在开始之前，我想提醒⼤家，今天的讨论将包含 1995 年《私⼈证券诉讼改⾰法案》安全港条款
下的前瞻性陈述。这些陈述涉及但不限于公司未来财务表现、商业战略、市场机遇、客⼾需求、
产品路线图、技术领先地位、供应链、运营模式，以及 2026 年第四季度和全年展望等⽅⾯。

前瞻性陈述基于我们当前的预期和假设，受⻛险和不确定因素影响，实际结果可能与陈述或暗⽰
的内容存在重⼤差异。相关⻛险已在我们向 SEC 提交的⽂件中予以说明，包括与 IPO 相关的最终
招股说明书及未来定期报告。除法律要求外，我们不承担更新这些前瞻性陈述的义务。
今天的电话会议中，我们还将讨论若⼲⾮ GAAP 财务指标。 GAAP 与⾮ GAAP 业绩的调节表已包
含在今⽇新闻稿及投资者关系⽹站上的补充材料中。
下⾯，我将会议移交给 Andrew 。
Andrew Feldman （⾸席执⾏官兼联合创始⼈）：谢谢 Sean ，也感谢⼤家今天的参与。
展望未来数⽉，我们看到两个确定性趋势：快速变化与算⼒短缺。过去 90 天⾥， AI ⽣态系统⻅
证了前沿模型领导者数度易主，数⼗款有价值的开源模型相继发布，重⼤安全事件相继发⽣，强
⼤的新型消费者智能体不断涌现，还有许多其他发展动态。
任何⼈都⽆法预测 90 天后 AI 的能⼒⾛向，更遑论⼀年之后。然⽽，在所有这些变化中，智能与
速度始终是每⼀个应⽤场景的两⼤核⼼要素 —— ⽆论是编程、安全，还是个⼈智能体。

在这个快速变化的世界中， Cerebras 的使命是确保⽆论 AI 如何发展， Cerebras 都能持续增⻓。
基础模型增⻓，我们增⻓；开源模型增⻓，我们增⻓；个⼈智能体增⻓，我们增⻓；⽆论是企业
还是初创公司增⻓，我们增⻓；⽆论客⼾需要硬件还是云计算资源，我们都能增⻓。我们的⽬标
是，⽆论什么因素驱动⾏业发展， Cerebras 都能赢得先机。
第三季度，我们在这⼀⽬标上取得了显著进展。
⾸先，我们超越了所有业绩指引⽬标。在对这些成绩感到满意的同时，我认为更重要的是 —— ⾃
上次财报电话会议以来，我们与多家 AI ⾏业领军企业达成了重要合作，最新进展是今天宣布与微
软签署了⼀项重要云服务协议。加上此前宣布的 AWS 和甲⻣⽂合作，迄今已有三家超⼤规模云
服务商选择将 Cerebras 平台纳⼊其基础架构战略。
这些新合作，叠加现有客⼾的持续增⻓，使我们得以全⾯上调第四季度核⼼业绩指引，并继续预
期 2027 年营收实现三倍增⻓，同时⽑利率持续扩张。 Bob 将在其发⾔部分详细介绍第三季度表

现及前瞻性指引。
关于业务结构

### Page 5

作为⼀家 AI 加速器公司，我们的核⼼创新在于芯⽚、系统和软件，使 AI 计算更快速。我们是⼀家
AI 硬件公司，拥有两条市场渠道。
第⼀条渠道是系统销售：将 Cerebras 硬件机架安装在客⼾的数据中⼼。这是⼀项优质业务，收
⼊确认快、回款快，但也具有⼀定的不规律性，取决于客⼾数据中⼼是否具备接收硬件的能⼒。
第⼆条渠道是我们的云服务。 AI 原⽣企业这⼀快速增⻓的客群，更倾向于消费算⼒（ token ），⽽
⾮⾃⾏部署硬件。鉴于数据中⼼和算⼒资源极为稀缺，我们同时拥有系统和数据中⼼，构成了重
要的战略优势。
对于⾃主⽣产硬件的公司⽽⾔， AI 云业务尤为出⾊。我们每兆⽡的成本远低于新兴云服务商，因
此能够实现更⾼的资本效率。两条渠道的业务⽐例会因季度⽽异，但我们相信两者都将随时间⼤
幅增⻓。
关于与 OpenAI 合作的市场猜测
OpenAI 是我们的重要⼤客⼾，也是紧密合作伙伴，我相信未来双⽅将继续保持这⼀关系。
OpenAI 在第三季度相较第⼆季度实现了增⻓，我们预计未来还将进⼀步增⻓。

OpenAI 基于 Cerebras 推出的 UltraFast SOL5-6 预览版取得了压倒性的成功，有⼒证明了市场对
快速 token 的需求极为巨⼤。
与此同时， Cerebras 确实没有⾜够的算⼒⽀持 UltraFast 在包括 Apsara 在内的更⼤范围内推出。
正如业界所述，我们认为算⼒是⾏业商业决策的关键驱动因素。事实上，即使 OpenAI 拥有海量
GPU 供应，仍需实施基于使⽤量的限制措施。
协议签署后，我们花费的时间⽐预期更⻓，才找到合同并上线满⾜协议要求所需的⼤量数据中⼼
增量产能。因此，我们⽬前在初始阶段的执⾏上有所滞后。
鉴于这⼀合作对我们业务的重要性，我们理解投资者对其给予⾼度关注。关于协议细节或相关新
闻，信息来源不太可能来⾃ Cerebras 或 OpenAI 以外的渠道。
例如，此前有传⾔称 Cerebras 出于技术原因⽆法⽀持 Astra ，这是错误的。 CS3 、 CS4 和 CS5 完
全有能⼒⽀持 Astra 级别的模型，乃⾄更⼤规模的模型。

同时，我预期并建议投资者预期， OpenAI 将利⽤多种合作关系和多样化硬件⽅案构建算⼒。
OpenAI 将淘汰旧模型，在这些计算资源上部署新模型，⽤于内部和外部⽤途。他们的算⼒需求
极为庞⼤，不同解决⽅案在其规模化进程中均有机会发挥作⽤。
关于推理速度的竞争地位
在此我想补充⼀点：尽管市场上存在各种实验性⽅案 —— 低批次 GPU 、初创公司发布、超⼤型
计算核⼼、超⼤规模云服务商的内部定制芯⽚ —— 我们依然是毫⽆争议的最快推理解决⽅案。我
们相信，我们的产品路线图将使我们在未来数年内保持这⼀优势。
业务评估框架：客⼾、算⼒、能⼒
我们对业务进展的评估框架保持不变，围绕三个维度进⾏：客⼾、算⼒和能⼒。
客⼾
我们正在快速拓展客⼾群，以扩⼤覆盖范围、多元化收⼊来源。第三季度是成果丰硕的⼀个季
度。我们赢得了两家新超⼤规模云服务商的合作。

们 2027 年和 2028 年的发展奠定了良好基础。
我们还与多家⾼质量新兴云服务商扩⼤了合作，包括 Firework 等，进⼀步多元化我们的市场渠
道，为客⼾提供更多访问 Cerebras ⾼速推理服务的途径。
开源权重模型同样为我们带来了助⼒，这些模型持续催⽣新应⽤，拓展 AI 在⽇常⽣活和各⾏业中
的应⽤边界。例如，就在本周，我们宣布 Cerebras 快速推理服务正在为 Built 公司的 OS 平台及个
⼈助⼿提供⽀持，为数百万⽤⼾带来极速推理体验。
通过 Built 这样的消费者应⽤，我们将 Cerebras 的强⼤算⼒直接送到⽤⼾⼿中。这正是快速 token

能够获得溢价的原因 —— 我们的云客⼾告诉我们，快速 token 能产⽣出⾊的⽑利率，⽽ Cerebras
的快速 token 是他们销售的利润最⾼的 token 。
算⼒
算⼒包含两个组成部分：数据中⼼算⼒和制造产能，两者对于业务增⻓都⾄关重要。
数据中⼼可⽤性是整个⾏业⾯临的持续性制约，我们也不例外。此外，我们认为美国政策环境的
演变可能会为美国境内的数据中⼼建设带来更⼤挑战。
2027 年，我们超过 75% 的数据中⼼产能将在美国境外上线。实际上，未来⼏个⽉内，加拿⼤、
法国和挪威的新设施将陆续投⼊运营。本季度，我们还签署了挪威和芬兰新增产能的合同，进⼀
步扩⼤了欧洲业务布局。同时，我们正处于在圣保罗签约数据中⼼的最后阶段，这将是我们在南
美洲的⾸个数据中⼼。
迄今为⽌，我们已为 2027 年底前交付签署了约 700 兆⽡的数据中⼼产能合同。我们的⾃有云已跻
⾝⾮超⼤规模云服务商中规模较⼤之列，⽬前正深⼊推进吉⽡级产能的谈判。
提升数据中⼼产能的最佳途径，是提升数据中⼼硬件的 token ⽣成能⼒，即每兆⽡产⽣更多

token。为此，分解式推理（ disaggregated inference ）通过将数据中⼼的 token 交付能⼒提升
最多 5 倍，有效扩⼤了我们的产能。每兆⽡产⽣更多超快 token ，意味着每个数据中⼼创造的收
⼊将显著增加。
在制造和供应链⽅⾯，我们正与 Flex 和 Sanmina 协⼒扩⼤制造规模，预计 2026 年底的制造产能
将超过年初的 10 倍。 2027 年，我们计划增设⼯⼚、进⼀步提升产能，以⽀撑预期的⼤幅增⻓。
我们的供应链也已做好⽀持这⼀增⻓的充分准备。
最重要的是，我们已获得台积电充⾜的晶圆产能承诺，台积电持续彰显其世界级合作伙伴的实
⼒。
同样值得关注的是我们供应链所不需要的东西 —— 这对我们同样⾄关重要。与其他竞争对⼿不
同，我们不使⽤ HBM 内存。我们认为，内存短缺及由此引发的价格上涨将持续⾄ 2028 年，这将
推⾼竞争对⼿的成本并限制其供应。⾏业性内存挑战为 Cerebras 创造了机遇。
⼯⼚和供应是扩⼤产能的必要条件，但并不充分，我们还必须保持⾼良率。

### Page 7

关于良率问题
Cerebras 发明了⼀种全新的计算架构。⽤针对其他架构设计的指标和思维模型来衡量我们，就
如同检查⼀辆特斯拉时追问排⽓管在哪⾥。晶圆级计算需要不同的参照框架，正如电动汽⻋需要
不同的评估维度⼀样。
我们欢迎就设计的各个⽅⾯提出问题，并将努⼒更清晰地分享架构细节。
然⽽，⾦融界存在⼀些持续流传的不实之词：⼀是声称我们的系统⽆法⽀持⼤型模型 —— 这显然
是错误的，因为我们⽬前已在运⾏⼤型模型；⼆是声称我们的制造良率低。两者均不属实。
关于模型规模问题，我已在前⾯的发⾔及此前的讨论中做出了回应。由于制造良率较难从外部观
察，我将在此直接加以说明。
良率分为两类：晶圆⼚良率和参数良率。我们来⾃台积电的晶圆⼚良率很⾼，参数良率同样如
此。我们的技术博客对此有详细介绍，以下做简要说明。
所有晶圆都含有缺陷。⼏⼗年来，这些缺陷是业界认为晶圆级处理器不可能实现的核⼼原因。我
们通过冗余设计解决了这⼀问题。
我们的设计包含约⼀百万个完全相同的计算单元（ tile ），其中⼀⼩部分作为备⽤。当缺陷导致某

个单元失效时，硬件会⾃动绕过它并激活备⽤单元。这⼀机制在晶圆⼚⽣产阶段和现场使⽤阶段
均有效，实现了⾼晶圆良率和强⼤的现场可靠性。
第⼆个挑战是参数变异（ parametric variation ）。即使硅功能正常，其电⽓特性也会在晶圆各处
存在差异 —— 在相同电压下，某些区域运⾏速度更快或更慢。这是芯⽚设计的基本现实，也是实
现晶圆级计算必须克服的障碍之⼀。
我们通过三种⽅式解决参数变异问题：
第⼀，  去除晶圆外缘。外缘是晶圆最薄弱的部分，也是参数变异的主要来源。我们使⽤适合 300
毫⽶晶圆的最⼤正⽅形区域，剔除⼤量边缘硅⽚。
第⼆，  针对宽泛的⼯况进⾏设计和测试。我们的核⼼能够容忍更⼤范围的变异。在时序收敛
（即验证电路满⾜时序要求的过程）阶段，我们在远⽐⾏业常规更宽泛的⼯作条件下对设计进⾏
验证，从⽽确保在晶圆各处变异的情况下仍能实现预期性能。
第三，  在晶圆各区域独⽴调节电压。我们发明了⼀种技术，可在晶圆的 84 个分区内独⽴调节核
⼼电压 —— 对较慢区域提⾼电压，对较快区域降低电压，从⽽对性能进⾏精确控制。据我们所

知，⽬前没有其他芯⽚具备这⼀能⼒。
最终结果是在晶圆⼚和⼯⼚两个环节均实现了极⾼的良率。我们通过⼯程化⼿段解决了其他⼈认
为使晶圆级计算不可能实现的问题，从⽽达成了这⼀成就。
最后补充⼀点：有⼈将我们硬件出货的相对较低⽑利率视为良率问题的证据，⽽实际上这仅仅反
映了我们制造能⼒的持续提升过程，以及在早期客⼾特殊定价协议下的最终批次出货情况。我们
预计，未来⼏个季度硬件⽑利率将⼤幅改善，最终超越 50% 的⻓期⽬标。
能⼒
我们在各个⽅⾯持续提升能⼒：新系统与处理器、改进的软件、先进的制造技术，以及提供⾏业
最佳解决⽅案的合作伙伴关系。

### Page 8

尽管 AI ⾏业变化节奏极快，推理速度的重要性仍在持续提升， Cerebras 依然是⽆可争议的领导
者。我们相信这⼀性能优势将⻓期持续，为此我们的产品路线图持续快速推进。
CS4 即将进⼊全⾯量产，性能和吞吐量均将翻倍。
我们的下⼀代处理器 —— 晶圆级引擎 5 （ WSE-5 ），已部署于第五代 CS5 系统，已从晶圆⼚如期
归来，甚⾄早于计划。 WSE-5 于 6 ⽉ 15 ⽇完成流⽚，芯⽚已回⼚启动，正在运⾏模型，并已进⼊
整机测试阶段。从流⽚到系统测试不⾜五个⽉，是出⾊的执⾏⼒体现。 CS5 的速度将⽐ CS4 提升
⼀倍以上，预计于 2027 年初夏进⼊量产。 CS5 基于台积电 5 纳⽶⼯艺节点，该节点的晶圆竞争相
对不那么激烈，晶圆成本也⼤幅降低。
我们的创新还延伸⾄完整解决⽅案领域。据我们所知， Cerebras 是⾸家实现⽣产级异构分解式
推理的公司。⽬前，我们的云服务正在使⽤ AMD GPU 处理预填充（ pre-fill ），使⽤ Cerebras
CS3 系统处理解码（ decode ）。为⽀持⽣产级上线，我们构建了⼀套分解式推理软件平台，兼容

AMD 及其他 GPU 产品，并将在 Cerebras 推理云及部分数据中⼼客⼾中部署同款⽅案。
分解式推理在相同功耗下能够提供更多⾼速 token ，这意味着云服务商每兆⽡获得更⾼利润， AI
⽤⼾获得更低的每 token 成本。我们相信分解式推理将成为主流云部署的默认配置之⼀。
我们的软件栈持续成熟，云服务实现了令⼈印象深刻的⾼可⽤性，系统运⾏速度不断提升，新模
型的启动时间也已缩短⾄仅需⼏天。
Cerebras 是 Kimi K3.1 的零⽇上线合作伙伴 ——Kimi K3.1 是⽉之暗⾯旗下的 2.8 万亿参数前沿级
开源权重模型，或许是⽬前市场上最顶尖的开源权重模型。 Cerebras 上运⾏的 Kimi K3.1 不仅是
业界最快，运⾏速度⾼达每秒 2000 个 token 。
⾛过近 11 年的历程，我们继续以惊⼈的速度创新。我们的团队在顺境与逆境中始终凝聚在⼀
起，公司内部的活⼒与激情从未如此⾼涨。
我们为解决⾏业中最艰难的问题⽽感到⾃豪。
总结：  我们超越所有指标，全⾯上调第四季度指引，并预期 2027 年营收三倍增⻓。我们在各核

⼼领域取得重要进展，包括新增两家超⼤规模云服务商客⼾，在过去六个⽉内已累计赢得三家超
⼤规模云服务商。 CS4 已开始出货，第五代处理器 WSE-5 已回⼚，并以创纪录的速度完成 CS5
整机测试。 AMD ⽣产级分解式推理已上线，对另⼀款 2.8 万亿参数模型的零⽇⽀持也以⾏业最佳
性能成功实现。

Bob Komin （⾼级副总裁兼⾸席财务官）：谢谢 Andrew ，⼤家下午好。在介绍第三季度业绩和
第四季度展望的具体内容之前，我想先回顾⼀下我们的业务规模化历程。
业务战略背景
我们选择在 2025 年初启动云服务，原因在于在这个快速演进的 AI 格局中， AI 原⽣企业希望快速
扩展算⼒，⽽不愿⾃建数据中⼼。由于超⼤规模云服务商会提前数年规划其数据中⼼算⼒分配，
往往⽆法为新硬件提供近期的⾃有设施空间。为了赢得基础模型实验室和超⼤规模云服务商所需
的⼤型部署合同， Cerebras 需要证明我们的系统能够在规模化场景下提供⾏业领先的推理性
能。在私有云中充分评估我们的解决⽅案之后，这些客⼾可以选择购买硬件，或采购我们的云服

务。
为了尽快提供⾜够的云算⼒以满⾜这些需求，我们选择尽快租⽤数据中⼼产能，将私有云规模向
吉⽡级推进。这⼀总体战略被证明是正确的 —— 数据中⼼产能⽬前已极为稀缺，⽽我们已有⼤量

### Page 9

产能在合同中锁定。
更重要的是，拥有私有云之后，我们得以向整个市场展⽰快速推理的价值，并进⼀步激发市场需
求。由于新型⼤型数据中⼼从⽴项到投⼊使⽤需要 15 ⾄ 18 个⽉，为了加快满⾜我们已有的⼤量
需求，我们选择从⼀家客⼾处回租了部分⾃有设备。如前所述，这在短期内对⽑利率造成了下⾏
压⼒，但也使我们能够在客⼾有需求时及时服务，从⽽拓展现有客⼾、赢得重要新客⼾。
如今，我们已赢得 OpenAI 等前沿实验室，以及 AWS 等超⼤规模云服务商，近期⼜新增了两家超
⼤规模云服务商客⼾。 Cognition 、 Figma 、 Block 、 Built 等领先客⼾也已加⼊我们的云平台。我
们以客⼾需求为导向，因地制宜 —— 有时销售硬件，有时提供云服务。
此前，我们也已说明， 2026 年将是⼀个建设之年 —— 扩⼤算⼒、提升能⼒、积累客⼾，为 2027
年营收实现三倍以上增⻓奠定基础。凭借今年迄今取得的强劲进展，我们有充分信⼼实现这⼀⽬
标。
第三季度业绩
现在转⼊第三季度业绩，我很⾼兴地宣布，第四季度将是我们增⻓和核⼼财务趋势的重要拐点。

我们已连续第三个季度超越核⼼财务指引。我们还提前实现了⾸次⾮ GAAP 净利润转正，早于预
期。
提⽰⼀下，核⼼指标的定义及其与 GAAP 的调节表已包含在今⽇财报发布中，并可在我们的⽹站
上查阅。
核⼼营收：  创历史新⾼，达 2.31 亿美元，环⽐增⻓ 10% ，同⽐增⻓ 70% ，超出我们 2.15 亿美元
指引中点 1600 万美元，超出幅度为 7% 。
核⼼云服务及其他服务营收：  同样创下历史新⾼，同⽐增⻓ 272% ，达 1.45 亿美元。第三季度云
服务营收环⽐增⻓ 13% 。我们⽬前预计第四季度云服务营收将基本持平。这⼀趋势并⾮由定价或
需求问题驱动 —— 定价环境依然健康，我们解决⽅案的需求持续远超供给。
云服务营收的增⻓瓶颈完全在于数据中⼼产能。我们将于 12 ⽉开始提升数据中⼼产能，为明年
积蓄动能，但遗憾的是时间太晚，⽆法对第四季度产⽣实质影响。我们预计 2027 年数据中⼼产
能将出现更为可观的增加，主要增量将从明年年中开始释放，届时我们将交付⽬前已在合同中锁

定的 700 兆⽡数据中⼼产能的较⼤部分。
核⼼硬件营收：  本季度为 8600 万美元，环⽐增⻓ 5% 。我们看到客⼾对快速推理服务的需求不断
增⻓，不仅来⾃我们的私有云，还来⾃与多家新客⼾签署的硬件采购合同。随着这些硬件发货计
划于第四季度启动，核⼼总营收将出现⼤幅跃升。
第三季度⾄第四季度的核⼼营收环⽐增⻓将接近 40% ，同⽐增⻓约为 85% 。这些硬件订单（包括
甲⻣⽂订单）将⾄少持续发货⾄ 2027 年上半年。
正如 Andrew 前⾯提到的，我们今天还宣布微软成为新客⼾。过去⼏个⽉，我们的销售管线规模
增⻓超过 10 倍，驱动因素涵盖多元化机遇，包括超⼤规模云服务商、新兴云服务商、主权云、
企业客⼾和 AI 原⽣公司等各类新⽼客⼾的需求。
⽑利率
第三季度核⼼⽑利率为 41% ，较第⼆季度环⽐改善 40 个基点，并⽐我们的季度指引⾼出 200 个基
点。

### Page 10

除了回租系统对⽑利率的影响外，本季度我们还⾯临⼀些逆⻛因素 —— 来⾃多年前签署的硬件采
购协议（采⽤较低定价）的最后⼀批发货正在消化。我们之前已指引，所有这些因素将造成暂时
性⽑利率下滑，低点将出现在第三季度。实际上，我们的表现优于预期 —— 低点已于上季度（第
⼆季度）出现。
现将第三季度核⼼⽑利率分拆如下：
核⼼硬件⽑利率  环⽐下降⾄ 31% ，主要由年前客⼾协议项下最后批次发货所致。我们⽬前正以
⼤幅提升后的现⾏价格执⾏硬件订单，这也意味着从第四季度起⽑利率将显著改善。
核⼼云服务及其他服务⽑利率  ⼤幅提升⾄ 46.9% ，较第⼆季度提升 510 个基点。主要驱动因素是
以更⾼定价和利润率承接的新业务。部分被从客⼾处回租数据中⼼产能和硬件的较⾼成本所抵
消。我们预计这⼀情况将⾄少持续⾄ 2027 年年中，直⾄我们拥有⾜够的⾃有数据中⼼产能来满
⾜较⾼⽔平的云需求。
综合以上情况，第四季度核⼼⽑利率预计将实现阶梯式改善，提升 700 个基点，⾄中点值 48% 。
我们预计核⼼总⽑利率将在近期超过 50% ，并持续向 60% 的⻓期⽬标推进。

盈利能⼒进展
第三季度及年初⾄今，我们在盈利能⼒⽅⾯取得了强劲进展。核⼼经营利润率环⽐改善 170 个基
点，亏损率降⾄ 14.3% ，超出指引中点近 10 个百分点。核⼼经营亏损与第⼆季度持平，约为
3300 万美元。核⼼净利润转正，达 3000 万美元。年初⾄今调整后核⼼ EBITDA 也实现正值，为
200 万美元。
随着第四季度营收和⽑利率⼤幅增⻓，我们预计所有核⼼关键盈利指标（包括经营利润率）将于
下季度转为正值。
剩余履约义务（ RPO ）与 OpenAI 合同
截⾄ 9 ⽉ 30 ⽇，剩余履约义务增⾄ 261 亿美元。我们按季度更新 RPO ，纳⼊新签业务，并根据剩
余承诺期内预计待确认⾦额的变化对现有合同价值进⾏调整。第三季度 RPO 不含 9 ⽉ 30 ⽇之后签
署的新合同影响，包括甲⻣⽂这⼀⼤额合同。
关于 OpenAI 合同的状态，投资者提问颇多，因为其占我们迄今已报告 RPO 的⼤部分。提⽰⼀
下，该合同已在 IPO 前随 S1 ⽂件提交，是⼀份 750 兆⽡算⼒的照付不议（ take-or-pay ）合同。

合同包含众多对双⽅均适⽤的条款，其中有⼀条规定：若我们在交付算⼒⽅⾯出现延误，
OpenAI 有权在此期间获得抵扣额度，⽤于未来的承诺采购。我们已计算潜在延误抵扣额度，并
已在 9 ⽉ 30 ⽇的 RPO 中予以扣除。
这些抵扣对营收和⽑利率的影响较⼩，原因有⼆：⼀是占 RPO 的⽐例相对较⼩；⼆是绝⼤部分
营收将在未来数年内按⽐例确认，抵扣⾦额将随之分摊。未来每个季度将根据最新估算进⾏更
新，并在必要时反映在指引中。
若算⼒交付延误持续达 90 天或以上， OpenAI 和 Cerebras 双⽅均有权取消该部分算⼒承诺（仅限
该部分）。我们估计今年可能⾯临取消⻛险的算⼒量，将低于总承诺兆⽡数的 5% 。
鉴于全球数据中⼼产能需求旺盛，我们在寻找并上线初始数据中⼼产能以满⾜交付计划⽅⾯，相
较极为紧张的时间表花费了额外时间。根据我们团队对各站点的频繁实地检查，我们⽬前预计将
于 2027 年下半年追回进度，届时未来延误抵扣⻛险应会相应降低。

### Page 11

我们采取了保守态度，未假设任何延误将因超出我们控制范围的外部依赖因素⽽被豁免。若豁免
情况发⽣，将减少我们所预估的延误抵扣⾦额。
此外，即便 OpenAI 或 Cerebras 选择取消某批次延误算⼒，我们认为凭借充⾜的市场需求，完全
可以找到其他客⼾承接。
OpenAI 还保留了在初始 750 兆⽡承诺之外，另⾏采购最多 1.25 吉⽡算⼒的选择权，最早可于
2029 年起上线。鉴于这还有三年时间，且 AI 领域变化之快，我们尚未就此展开讨论。
资产负债表与资本效率
我们继续保持强劲的流动性。第三季度末，现⾦、现⾦等价物、受限现⾦及有价证券合计约 78
亿美元。我们还拥有最⾼ 8.5 亿美元的循环信贷额度，⽬前尚未动⽤。
今年 1 ⽉获得的 10 亿美元预付流动资⾦贷款，以及作为 OpenAI 合同部分内容⽤于新数据中⼼产
能装修的⾮经常性直通费⽤的持续收款，已为今年迄今⼤部分净营运资本需求和资本⽀出提供了
资⾦。
我们不是⼀家新兴云服务商，⽽是业界最快推理解决⽅案的垂直整合发明者。我们的业务具备技

术差异化所赋予的韧性，我们相信这将在⻓期内驱动更⾼的利润潜⼒和现⾦创造能⼒。
我们的模式在已部署算⼒设备的资本投⼊⽅⾯也较低。对于我们在数据中⼼⾃主制造的⼤量设备
—— 与其他选择⾃研并部署 AI 芯⽚的公司类似 —— 我们⽆需向第三⽅⽀付 70% ⽑利率，也⽆需使
⽤⾼价 HBM ，只需⽀付我们的物料清单（ BOM ）成本。这既降低了每兆⽡的相对资本⽀出，⼜
使我们能够⽣产最快速、最有价值的 token 。
这反映在我们的资本结构中 —— 尽管我们私有云建设规模快速扩张，资产负债表上唯⼀的债务仅
为 OpenAI 流动资⾦贷款。我们还持续从客⼾处获得预付款，这是提升资本效率的另⼀杠杆。
我们当前的资本结构包含充裕的现⾦和投资，负债极少，为我们以多种⽅式持续融资、⽀撑快速
增⻓提供了充分灵活性 —— 这在当前资本市场环境下尤为有利。
展望
2026 年第四季度展望：
核⼼营收： 3.10 亿⾄ 3.30 亿美元

2026 年全年展望（上调）：

核⼼营收： 9.40 亿⾄ 9.60 亿美元

核⼼经营利润率： -10% ⾄ -8%
总结

### Page 12

第三季度是 Cerebras ⼜⼀创纪录的季度，第四季度亦将如此。我们灵活的硬件与云服务兼售模
式正在推动营收强劲增⻓，⽑利率改善也⽐预期提前了整整⼀个季度。
我们在制造和数据中⼼产能规模化⽅⾯取得了显著进展，这将使我们能够在 2027 年实现营收三
倍增⻓，同时核⼼⽑利率向 60% 的⻓期⽬标持续推进。我们已展现出强劲的运营杠杆效应，并预
计第四季度核⼼经营利润将基本实现盈亏平衡，此后预计在 2027 年⼤幅向 40% 的⻓期经营利润
率⽬标迈进。
这种规模、增⻓与盈利能⼒的综合优势，使我们成为独具特⾊的领先 AI 系统提供商。
最后补充⼀点：提醒⼤家，我们上市已逾 180 天， IPO 股票锁定期已于 11 ⽉ 9 ⽇全部届满解锁。

明⽩，谢谢。后续问题是：我们看到前沿 AI 实验室不仅能快速研发硬件（⽐如 Jalapeno 项⽬），

### Page 13

Andrew Feldman ：
这是个⾮常好的问题。 Profit 公司在其 S1 或某条推⽂中提到，他们只⽤了约三天时间完成了 AMD
GPU 的迁移上线。
这是⼀个⾮常有意义且重要的进展，尤其对于⻓期视⻆的观察者⽽⾔。我认为，业界尚未充分认
识到这对我们⽣态系统意味着什么。
编译器和底层软件历来是新架构⾯临的根本障碍，它们天然有利于在位者，也是⼈们常说的 " 软
件护城河 " 的核⼼来源。如果 Profit 公司真能实现他们所说的，那就意味着底层软件的价值将收
缩，并随时间趋近于消失。软件护城河也将随之收窄并消弭。
若底层软件的价值趋于零，真正能够提供价值的，就只剩下创新硬件。这意味着竞争分化将更多
取决于硬件的差异化程度。
我认为，这对 Cerebras 这样的创新硬件公司 —— 以及其他采⽤全新架构的硬件先⾏者 —— 是巨
⼤的优势，同时将削弱在位者⻓期以来享有的优势地位。
这个问题问得很好，值得整个⾏业密切关注。谢谢。

Atif Malik （花旗）：
谢谢接受提问。Andrew ，我有个关于产品路线图的问题： CS4 与 CS3 的交叉点预计何时出现？
是 2027 年吗？另外， CS4 在吞吐量上有了显著提升，如果沿着这⼀路径推进到 CS5 及后续产
品，在某个节点上，贵公司的芯⽚是否能单独提供⾜够快的速度和吞吐量，⽆需搭配 GPU 使
⽤？
Andrew Feldman ：
Atif ，你好，很⾼兴和你交流。⾸先，就在⼏周前，我们所有的部署都是单独使⽤ CS3 的，只是
最近才开始部署分解式推理⽅案，由此带来了吞吐量的显著提升，⾮常令⼈振奋。这是第⼀个观
察。
可⻅， CS3 单独使⽤效果已经很好，但分解式推理（我们⽬前正在部署，已进⼊正式可⽤阶段）
在⾼速 token 受限的情况下，能够产⽣更多⾼速 token 。
关于 CS3 与 CS4 的交叉点，我预计将在 2027 年第⼀季度出现。产品路线图⽬前看起来⾮常好，
CS5 进度超前。虽然我在准备发⾔中已提及，但从流⽚到芯⽚完成整机测试仅⽤五个⽉，确实是

⾮凡的执⾏成果。 CS5 预计将于 2027 年初夏开始量产，性能⽐ CS4 翻倍，⽽ CS4 性能⽐ CS3 翻
倍。
Atif Malik ：
很好。 Bob ，关于第四季度云营收指引持平的情况：在 2027 年营收三倍增⻓的⽬标中，能否帮
助我们理解云与硬件的业务结构？
Bob Komin ：
我认为理解硬件业务结构的关键在于，这类订单的规模可以⾮常⼤。我们希望在未来⼀两个季度
内提供⼀些关于发货情况的指引。这在很⼤程度上取决于客⼾数据中⼼是否准备就绪。可以说的
是，第四季度和进⼊第⼀季度，硬件占⽐将⽐过去⼏个季度更接近 50% 。
⾄于 2027 年其余时间的情况，在第四季度财报电话会议时我们会有更多信息，届时将对 2027 年
整体情况提供更完整的展望。
Atif Malik ：谢谢。
接线员：谢谢。下⼀个问题来⾃ Needham 的 Quinn Bolton 。请提问。
Quinn Bolton （ Needham ）：
⼤家好，祝贺取得进展。 Andrew ，我想确认⼀下我理解是否正确：在 RPO 和您的假设中，您已

经就潜在延误抵扣做了预提，但⽬前尚未收到任何关于延误算⼒的取消通知，是这样吗？
Andrew Feldman：正确。
Quinn Bolton：
好的，很好。第⼆个问题：市场上有⼀种担忧，认为低批次 GPU 能够实现快速推理。听起来那
是⼀种成本很⾼的 token 交付⽅式。能否⽐较⼀下 Cerebras 与低批次 GPU 配置的每 token 成本？
Andrew Feldman ：

### Page 15

当然。 GPU 可以通过降低批次规模来换取更⾼的速度，但代价是牺牲吞吐量 —— 低批次下吞吐
量很低，⽽当你试图提⾼速度时， GPU 会进⼊低批次区间，总吞吐量变得⾮常⼩。
这是由架构特性决定的。可以这样理解： GPU 通过降批次确实能获得⼀定程度的性能提升，但
这样做时，每 token 成本会急剧上升 —— 同时服务的⽤⼾数⼤幅减少，总 token ⽣产量极为有限。
随着速度要求进⼀步提⾼，我们的优势会越来越强。当速度达到 GPU ⽆论如何都⽆法提供的⽔
平时， Cerebras 是唯⼀的选项 —— 在批次为 1 的情况下， GPU 仍然⽆法与我们媲美。
关于成本交叉点：⼤约在每秒 250 ⾄ 300 个 token 时，我们在经济性上更有优势；到每秒 500 、
800 、 1000 个 token 时，我们的优势会远超对⼿；在 800 ⾄ 2000 个 token 的区间， GPU 根本⽆法参
与竞争。这就是当前竞争格局的基本⾯貌。这个问题涉及复杂话题，提得很好。
Quinn Bolton：谢谢， Andrew 。

接线员：感谢提问。下⼀个问题来⾃ TD Cowen 的 Joshua Bruckheimer 。请提问。
Joshua Bruckheimer （ TD Cowen ）：
⼤家好，感谢接受提问，并祝贺微软合作顺利落地。⾸先，请谈谈在⼟地、电⼒和建筑问题的背
景下，您是如何分配算⼒的？看起来硅⽚和机架供应不是问题，那么在增量硬件销售⽅⾯还有多
⼤ 空 间 ？ 另 外 ， 鉴 于 云 客 ⼾ ⾯ 临 ⼟ 地 、 电 ⼒ 和 建 筑⽅ ⾯ 的 挑 战 ， 是 否 与 OpenAI 讨 论 过 由
OpenAI 或其合作伙伴购买硬件并部署在他们⾃⼰的数据中⼼？谢谢。
Andrew Feldman：这个问题确实有两层。 Josh ，第⼀层是：⼟地、电⼒和建筑及其施⼯是否
真的是限制因素？答案是肯定的。台积电已向我们做出了 2027 年充⾜晶圆产能的承诺，我们对
此⾮常有把握。不使⽤ HBM 显然也是极⼤的优势，因为 HBM ⽬前供应极为紧张。
你第⼆层问题问得很好：这是否为我们在数据中⼼建设期间销售硬件创造了机会？唯⼀需要注意

的是，据我们所知，⼏乎所有⼈的数据中⼼建设都在延期 —— 包括 OpenAI ，包括我们知道的所
有超⼤规模云服务商数据中⼼。数据中⼼延期是全球性的普遍现象，并⾮我们独有，我们的客⼾
同样⾯临这⼀制约。所以，答案是：是的，我们有产品供应；是的，我们正在向拥有数据中⼼的
客⼾发货；但同样，当前数据中⼼普遍延期，确实影响了实际进展。是否清楚了？
Joshua Bruckheimer ：
明⽩，但三问中的最后⼀个，也是我提的问题⾥⽐较难听的⼀部分 ——OpenAI 合同是否有可能
转为硬件采购？
Andrew Feldman ：
抱歉，合同中确实赋予了他们这⼀权利，但据我们了解，他们⾃⼰的数据中⼼建设同样落后于计
划。
Joshua Bruckheimer ：好的，明⽩了。谢谢。那么跟进问题 ——
Andrew Feldman ：Josh ，这已经是第七问了。
Joshua Bruckheimer：确实我的问题，我承认。能否谈谈甲⻣⽂和微软是如何向他们的客⼾定
位 Cerebras 的产品和服务？这是否主要体现为硬件销售，然后根据超快推理需求来驱动客⼾使

⽤量？谢谢。
